that substitution followed by rescaling by $\phi$ reproduces the tiling.
Separate exact inflation symmetry from ordinary translation symmetry.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Cut-and-project order and diffraction}

\begin{definition}[Cut-and-project sets]
Let $\mathcal L$ be a lattice in
$\R^d\times\R^m$, with projections $\pi_\parallel$ and $\pi_\perp$ onto
physical and internal space.  A \emph{cut-and-project scheme} requires
$\pi_\parallel$ to be injective on $\mathcal L$ and
$\pi_\perp(\mathcal L)$ to be dense.  For a compact window
$W\subset\R^m$ with nonempty interior, define the \emph{model set}
\[
\Lambda(W)=\{\pi_\parallel(x):x\in\mathcal L,
\ \pi_\perp(x)\in W\}.
\]
It is \emph{regular} when the boundary of $W$ has measure zero.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[The Fibonacci chain by cut and project]
Let $\phi'=-1/\phi$ and embed $\Z^2$ by
\[
(m,n)\longmapsto(m+n\phi,\,m+n\phi')
\in\R_\parallel\times\R_\perp.
\]
Prove the injectivity and density conditions in the definition.  Choose a
generic closed interval window of length $1+1/\phi$, with boundary
disjoint from the projected lattice, and order the resulting
physical points.  Determine the two possible nearest-neighbor spacings and
show, after an overall rescaling and a possible exchange of letters, that
their sequence is a Fibonacci tiling.  Track how translating the window
changes the local pattern without changing its set of finite patches.
Interpret the internal coordinate as an arithmetic phase invisible in
physical space.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Autocorrelation and diffraction]
For a uniformly discrete point set $\Lambda\subset\R^d$, its Dirac comb is
$\omega=\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\delta_x$.  When the vague limit exists, its
\emph{autocorrelation} is
\[
\gamma=\lim_{R\to\infty}
 \frac{\omega|_{B_R}*\widetilde{\omega|_{B_R}}}{\vol(B_R)},
\qquad
\widetilde\mu(f)=\overline{\mu(\widetilde f)},
\quad \widetilde f(x)=\overline{f(-x)}.
\]
The positive measure $\widehat\gamma$ is its \emph{diffraction measure}; its
atoms are Bragg peaks.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Sharp diffraction without periodicity]
For a finite sample $\Lambda_R$, show that the kinematic scattering
intensity is
\[
I_R(k)=\left|\sum_{x\in\Lambda_R}e^{-ik\cdot x}\right|^2
\]
and relate its volume-normalized limit to $\widehat\gamma$.  Recover the
ordinary reciprocal lattice for a periodic crystal.  For Fibonacci
approximants obtained from the first $F_n$ tiles, compute $I_R(k)$ on a grid
of wave numbers and track the largest peaks as $n$ increases.  Express the
observed peak locations as integer combinations of $1$ and $1/\phi$, and
verify directly that no finite reciprocal lattice contains all of them.

Compare this mechanism with the sharp noncrystallographic diffraction
reported in
\href{https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevLett.53.1951}
{Shechtman et al., \emph{Physical Review Letters}
\textbf{53} (1984)}.  State what diffraction establishes---long-range
coherent order and its symmetry---and what it does not establish without a
structural model.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Quasicrystal spectra and gap labelling}

\begin{definition}[The Fibonacci Hamiltonian and integrated density of states]
Let $\alpha=1/\phi$ and
\[
v_\theta(n)=\mathbf1_{[1-\alpha,1)}
 \bigl(\theta+n\alpha\bmod1\bigr).
\]
The \emph{Fibonacci Hamiltonian} on $\ell^2(\Z)$ is
\[
(H_\theta\psi)_n=\psi_{n+1}+\psi_{n-1}
 +\lambda v_\theta(n)\psi_n.
\]
For the uniquely ergodic rotation of $\theta$, its \emph{integrated density
of states} is
\[
N(E)=\int_0^1
 \langle\delta_0,
 \mathbf1_{(-\infty,E]}(H_\theta)\delta_0\rangle\,d\theta.
\]
It is the limiting number of states below $E$ per lattice site.

Let $\Omega$ be the closure of the shift orbit of a Fibonacci sequence in
$\{0,1\}^{\Z}$, and let $S:\Omega\to\Omega$ be the shift.  The associated
observable algebra is the crossed product
$\mathcal A_\alpha=C(\Omega)\rtimes_S\Z$, generated by $C(\Omega)$ and a
unitary $U$ satisfying $UfU^*=f\circ S^{-1}$.  The unique invariant measure
$\mu$ gives the canonical trace
\[
\tau\!\left(\sum_n f_nU^n\right)=\int_\Omega f_0\,d\mu.
\]
The Hamiltonians above are the covariant representations of one element of
this algebra.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Diophantine trace labels]
Prove that $N(E)$ is nondecreasing and is constant on every open spectral
gap.  Approximate $\alpha$ by Fibonacci convergents and interpret the finite
periodic approximants as large-unit-cell crystals.  Define the additive
Fibonacci label group and its allowed label set by
\[
 \Gamma_\alpha=\Z+\alpha\Z,
 \qquad
 \mathcal L_\alpha=\Gamma_\alpha\cap[0,1].
\]
Use the substitution recurrence to show that the measures of one-letter and
two-letter cylinder sets belong to $\Z+\alpha\Z$.  Prove the same for every
finite disjoint union of cylinder sets.  For the corresponding projections
in $C(\Omega)\subset\mathcal A_\alpha$, compute their traces and identify
them with elements of $\mathcal L_\alpha$.  Compare these values with the
plateaus of the finite-approximant integrated density of states, without
asserting that every allowed label is an open spectral gap.
\end{exercise}

\section{The Heisenberg Group}

\subsection{Central extension}

\begin{definition}[Heisenberg group]
For a symplectic space $(E,\omega)$, its \emph{Heisenberg group} is
\[
\mathbb H(E,\omega)=E\times\R
\]
with multiplication
\[
(z,s)(z',s')
=(z+z',s+s'+\tfrac12\omega(z,z')).
\]
Write
$\mathbb H_n=\mathbb H(\R^n\oplus(\R^n)^*,\omega)$ for the canonical
symplectic form on $\R^n\oplus(\R^n)^*$.
For $E=V\oplus V^*$ and $\lambda\ne0$, the \emph{Schr\"odinger
representation} on $L^2(V)$ is
\[
\bigl(\pi_\lambda(q,p,s)\xi\bigr)(x)
=e^{i\lambda(s+p(x-q/2))}\xi(x-q).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Central extensions and projective representations]
Prove that $\mathbb H(E,\omega)$ is a locally compact group with center
$\{0\}\times\R$ and that
\[
\pi_\lambda(0,s)=e^{i\lambda s}I.
\]
Prove that projective representations of $E$ with multiplier
$e^{i\lambda\omega/2}$ correspond to representations of
$\mathbb H(E,\omega)$ with central character $e^{i\lambda s}$.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Stone--von Neumann theorem}

\begin{exercise}[Stone--von Neumann theorem]
Prove that $\pi_\lambda$ is irreducible.  Prove that every strongly
continuous irreducible unitary representation of
$\mathbb H(V\oplus V^*,\omega)$ with central character $e^{i\lambda s}$,
$\lambda\ne0$, is unitarily equivalent to $\pi_\lambda$.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Weyl quantization}

\begin{definition}[Weyl quantization]
For $a\in\mathcal S(\R^{2n})$, define
$\operatorname{Op}_\hbar^W(a):\mathcal S(\R^n)\to\mathcal S(\R^n)$ by
\[
\bigl(\operatorname{Op}_\hbar^W(a)\psi\bigr)(x)
=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^n}
\int_{\R^n\times\R^n}
e^{i(x-y)\cdot p/\hbar}
a\!\left(\frac{x+y}{2},p\right)\psi(y)\,dy\,dp.
\]
For polynomial symbols, use the same formula as an oscillatory integral on
$\mathcal S(\R^n)$.
For the coordinate functions $q_j$ and $p_j$, set
\[
Q_j=\operatorname{Op}_\hbar^W(q_j),
\qquad P_j=\operatorname{Op}_\hbar^W(p_j).
\]
The canonical Poisson bracket on $\R^{2n}$ is
\[
\{a,b\}
=\sum_{j=1}^n
\left(
\frac{\partial a}{\partial q_j}\frac{\partial b}{\partial p_j}
-\frac{\partial a}{\partial p_j}\frac{\partial b}{\partial q_j}
\right).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Wigner distributions]
For $\psi,\varphi\in\mathcal S(\R^n)$, define
\[
W_\hbar(\psi,\varphi)(q,p)
=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^n}
\int_{\R^n}e^{-ip\cdot y/\hbar}
\overline{\psi(q-y/2)}\,\varphi(q+y/2)\,dy.
\]
Write $W_\hbar[\psi]=W_\hbar(\psi,\psi)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Weyl calculus and the Moyal product]
Prove on $\mathcal S(\R^n)$ that
\[
Q_j\psi(x)=x_j\psi(x),
\qquad P_j\psi(x)=-i\hbar\partial_j\psi(x),
\qquad [Q_j,P_k]=i\hbar\delta_{jk}I.
\]
Derive the Weyl relations and recover the Schr\"odinger representation of
the Heisenberg group.  Prove
\[
\operatorname{Op}_\hbar^W(a)^*
=\operatorname{Op}_\hbar^W(\overline a),
\qquad
\|\operatorname{Op}_\hbar^W(a)\|_{\rm HS}
=(2\pi\hbar)^{-n/2}\|a\|_2,
\]
and
\[
\langle\psi,\operatorname{Op}_\hbar^W(a)\varphi\rangle
=\int_{\R^{2n}}a(q,p)W_\hbar(\psi,\varphi)(q,p)\,dq\,dp.
\]
Prove that for $a,b\in\mathcal S(\R^{2n})$ there is a unique
$a\star_\hbar b\in\mathcal S(\R^{2n})$ satisfying
\[
\operatorname{Op}_\hbar^W(a\star_\hbar b)
=\operatorname{Op}_\hbar^W(a)\operatorname{Op}_\hbar^W(b).
\]
Derive the oscillatory-integral formula for $\star_\hbar$ and prove
\[
a\star_\hbar b
=ab+\frac{i\hbar}{2}\{a,b\}+O(\hbar^2),
\qquad
\frac{a\star_\hbar b-b\star_\hbar a}{i\hbar}
=\{a,b\}+O(\hbar^2)
\]
in the Schwartz-symbol topology.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Groenewold--van Hove obstruction]
Prove that Weyl quantization carries Poisson brackets to
$(i\hbar)^{-1}$ times commutators exactly for affine and quadratic
polynomials.  Quantize $q^2p^2$ using
\[
q^2p^2=\frac19\{q^3,p^3\}
=\frac13\{q^2p,qp^2\}
\]
and prove that there is no linear quantization of the polynomial Poisson
algebra satisfying
\[
\mathcal Q(1)=I,\qquad
\mathcal Q(q)=Q,\qquad
\mathcal Q(p)=P,\qquad
\mathcal Q(q^m)=Q^m,\qquad
\mathcal Q(p^m)=P^m,
\]
together with
\[
\mathcal Q(\{f,g\})=(i\hbar)^{-1}
[\mathcal Q(f),\mathcal Q(g)]
\]
on a common invariant domain.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Heisenberg Plancherel theory}

\begin{exercise}[Heisenberg Plancherel and inversion]
\label{ex:heisenberg-harmonic-analysis}
For $V=\R^n$ and
$f\in L^1(\mathbb H_n)\cap L^2(\mathbb H_n)$, use Haar measure
$dq\,dp\,ds$ and the scalar Fourier transform
$\widehat h(\lambda)=\int_\R h(s)e^{-i\lambda s}\,ds$, and define
\[
\widehat f(\lambda)
=\int_{\mathbb H_n}f(g)\pi_\lambda(g)^*\,dg.
\]
Derive the Plancherel measure
$(2\pi)^{-(n+1)}|\lambda|^n\,d\lambda$ and prove
\[
\|f\|_2^2
=(2\pi)^{-(n+1)}\int_{\R\setminus\{0\}}
\|\widehat f(\lambda)\|_{\rm HS}^2|\lambda|^n\,d\lambda.
\]
Prove the inversion formula and the distributional orthogonality of Weyl
operators.  Prove that representations with trivial central character have
zero Plancherel measure.
\end{exercise}

\part{Averaging}

\section{Linear Spaces, Tensor Products, and Duality}

\begin{definition}[Linear spaces and maps]
Let $k$ be $\R$ or $\C$.  A \emph{linear space} over $k$ is an abelian group
$V$ with scalar multiplication satisfying
\[
a(v+w)=av+aw,\quad (a+b)v=av+bv,\quad
(ab)v=a(bv),\quad 1v=v.
\]
A map $T:V\to W$ is \emph{linear} when
$T(av+bw)=aT(v)+bT(w)$.  Its kernel and image are
\[
\ker T=\{v:T(v)=0\},\qquad \im T=\{T(v):v\in V\}.
\]
For $T:V\to V$, a scalar $\lambda$ is an \emph{eigenvalue} when
$\ker(T-\lambda I)\ne0$; a nonzero vector in this kernel is an
\emph{eigenvector}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Dual spaces and tensor products]
The \emph{dual space} of $V$ is
$V^\vee=\Hom_k(V,k)$.  Its canonical evaluation pairing is
\[
\operatorname{ev}_V:V^\vee\times V\longrightarrow k,
\qquad (\varphi,v)\longmapsto\varphi(v).
\]
The algebraic transpose of $T:V\to W$ is
$T^\vee:W^\vee\to V^\vee$, defined by $T^\vee(\varphi)=\varphi\circ T$.

The \emph{tensor product} $V\otimes W$ is characterized by a bilinear map
$(v,w)\mapsto v\otimes w$ with the following universal property: every
bilinear map $b:V\times W\to X$ factors uniquely as
\[
V\times W\longrightarrow V\otimes W
\xrightarrow{\,\widetilde b\,}X.
\]
The evaluation pairing induces a linear map
$\operatorname{ev}_V:V^\vee\otimes V\to k$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Dualizability and finite dimensionality]
A linear space $V$ is \emph{dualizable} when there is a coevaluation map
\[
\operatorname{coev}_V:k\longrightarrow V\otimes V^\vee
\]
satisfying the two snake identities
\[
(\id_V\otimes\operatorname{ev}_V)
\circ(\operatorname{coev}_V\otimes\id_V)=\id_V,
\]
\[
(\operatorname{ev}_V\otimes\id_{V^\vee})
\circ(\id_{V^\vee}\otimes\operatorname{coev}_V)=\id_{V^\vee}.
\]
A linear space is \emph{finite-dimensional} if it is dualizable.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Categorical trace and dimension]
Let $V$ be dualizable, let
$s_{V,V^\vee}:V\otimes V^\vee\to V^\vee\otimes V$ exchange the factors,
and let $T\in\End(V)$.  The \emph{categorical trace} is the scalar
\[
\operatorname{tr}(T)
=\operatorname{ev}_V\circ s_{V,V^\vee}
 \circ(T\otimes\id_{V^\vee})\circ\operatorname{coev}_V,
\qquad
\dim V=\operatorname{tr}(\id_V).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Dualizability recovers ordinary finite dimension]
Construct $V\otimes W$ as a quotient of the free linear space on
$V\times W$ and prove its universal property.  If $V$ has basis
$e_1,\ldots,e_n$ with dual basis $e^1,\ldots,e^n$, show that
\[
\operatorname{coev}_V(1)=\sum_{j=1}^n e_j\otimes e^j
\]
is independent of the chosen basis and satisfies the snake identities.
Conversely, write any proposed coevaluation as
$\operatorname{coev}_V(1)=\sum_{j=1}^r v_j\otimes\varphi_j$ and use a snake
identity to prove that the $v_j$ span $V$.  Conclude that dualizability is
equivalent to ordinary finite dimensionality.  Finally prove
\[
\operatorname{tr}(T)=\sum_{j=1}^n e^j(Te_j),\qquad
\operatorname{tr}(ST)=\operatorname{tr}(TS),\qquad
\dim V=n.
\]
\end{exercise}

\section{Hilbert Spaces}

\begin{definition}[Hilbert spaces and orthogonality]
An \emph{inner product} on a complex linear space is conjugate-linear in its
first argument, linear in its second, positive definite, and satisfies
$\langle x,y\rangle=\overline{\langle y,x\rangle}$.  Its norm is
$\|x\|=\sqrt{\langle x,x\rangle}$.  A \emph{Hilbert space} is an inner-product
space complete for this norm.

Vectors are \emph{orthogonal} when their inner product is zero.  For a
subspace $M\subseteq H$, define
\[
M^\perp=\{x:\langle m,x\rangle=0\text{ for every }m\in M\}.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Bounded operators and adjoints]
A linear map $T:H\to K$ between Hilbert spaces is \emph{bounded} when
\[
\|T\|=\sup_{\|x\|\leq1}\|Tx\|<\infty.
\]
An \emph{adjoint} of $T$ is an operator $T^*:K\to H$ satisfying
\[
\langle x,Ty\rangle=\langle T^*x,y\rangle.
\]
An operator is self-adjoint when $T=T^*$, unitary when $T^*T=TT^*=I$, and
normal when $T^*T=TT^*$.  An \emph{orthogonal projection} is an operator
$P$ satisfying $P=P^*=P^2$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Hilbert-space foundations]
Prove the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, the projection theorem, and the Riesz
representation theorem.  Deduce the existence of adjoints and prove
\[
\|T\|^2=\|T^*T\|,\qquad
(\im T)^\perp=\ker T^*.
\]
For every closed subspace $M\subseteq H$, prove
$H=M\oplus M^\perp$ and construct the orthogonal projection $P_M:H\to M$.
Show that an orthonormal family $(e_j)$ is complete exactly when
$\|x\|^2=\sum_j|\langle e_j,x\rangle|^2$ for every $x\in H$.
\end{exercise}

\section{Measure and Probability}

\begin{definition}[Measure spaces and Haar measure]
A \emph{$\sigma$-algebra} $\mathcal F$ on a set $X$ contains
$\varnothing$ and is closed under complements and countable unions.  A
\emph{measure} is a countably additive map
$\mu:\mathcal F\to[0,\infty]$ with $\mu(\varnothing)=0$, and
$(X,\mathcal F,\mu)$ is a
\emph{measure space}.  A map is \emph{measurable} when inverse images of
measurable sets are measurable.  For $1\leq p<\infty$, define
\[
L^p(X,\mu)=
\left\{f:\int_X|f|^p\,d\mu<\infty\right\}\big/\text{equality a.e.}
\]
with its usual norm, and let $L^\infty(X,\mu)$ consist of essentially
bounded measurable functions.

A \emph{topological group} is a group with a Hausdorff topology for which
multiplication and inversion are continuous.  It is \emph{locally compact}
when every point has a compact neighborhood.  A \emph{Radon measure} is a
Borel measure finite on compact sets, outer regular on Borel sets, and inner
regular on open sets.  A \emph{left Haar measure} on a locally compact group
is a nonzero left-invariant Radon measure, so
$\mu(gE)=\mu(E)$.  Its \emph{modular function} is determined by
\[
\mu(Eg)=\Delta_G(g)^{-1}\mu(E).
\]
Write $C_b(G)$, $C_0(G)$, and $C_c(G)$ for the
continuous functions that are bounded, vanish at infinity, and have
compact support, respectively.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Probability spaces and convergence]
A \emph{probability space} is a measure space
$(\Omega,\mathcal F,\mathbb P)$ with $\mathbb P(\Omega)=1$.  A
\emph{random variable} is a measurable map $X:\Omega\to Y$, its
\emph{law} is $\mathbb P_X(B)=\mathbb P(X^{-1}(B))$, and, when it is
scalar-valued and integrable, its \emph{expectation} is its integral.
A random vector $X=(X_1,\ldots,X_N)$ has \emph{joint density} $p$ with
respect to a measure $\mu$ when
\[
\mathbb P(X\in B)=\int_Bp\,d\mu.
\]
The Gaussian law $N(m,v)$ on $\R$ has density
\[
\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi v}}\exp\!\left[-\frac{(x-m)^2}{2v}\right].
\]
A sequence of random variables in a metric space
$(Y,d)$ \emph{converges in probability} to $X$ when
\[
\mathbb P(d(X_N,X)>\varepsilon)\longrightarrow0
\qquad(\varepsilon>0).
\]
It \emph{converges in distribution}, written $X_N\Longrightarrow X$,
when
\[
\int f\,d\mathbb P_{X_N}\longrightarrow\int f\,d\mathbb P_X
\]
for every bounded continuous $f$.
\end{definition}

\section{\texorpdfstring{$C^*$-Algebras and Functional Calculus}{C*-Algebras and Functional Calculus}}

\begin{definition}[$C^*$-algebras, spectrum, and positivity]
A \emph{$C^*$-algebra} is a complete normed complex algebra $A$ with a
conjugate-linear involution satisfying
\[
(ab)^*=b^*a^*,\qquad \|ab\|\leq\|a\|\|b\|,\qquad
\|a^*a\|=\|a\|^2.
\]
For a unital $C^*$-algebra, define
\[
\spec_A(a)=\{\lambda\in\C:a-\lambda1\text{ is not invertible}\}.
\]
An element is \emph{positive}, written $a\geq0$, when $a=b^*b$ for some
$b\in A$.  It is \emph{self-adjoint} when $a=a^*$ and \emph{normal} when
$a^*a=aa^*$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Gelfand spectrum]
For a commutative $C^*$-algebra $A$, let $\widehat A$ be the space of
nonzero homomorphisms $\chi:A\to\C$ with its weak-$*$ topology.  The
\emph{Gelfand transform} is
\[
\Gamma:A\longrightarrow C_0(\widehat A),
\qquad \Gamma(a)(\chi)=\chi(a).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Spectrum, Gelfand duality, and functional calculus]
Prove that $\spec_A(a)$ is nonempty and compact and that
\[
r(a)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\|a^n\|^{1/n}.
\]
For normal $a$, prove $r(a)=\|a\|$.  Prove that positive elements have
unique positive square roots and that $a\geq0$ if and only if $a=a^*$ and
$\spec_A(a)\subseteq[0,\infty)$.  Prove that the Gelfand transform is an
isometric $*$-isomorphism.  For every normal element $a$ of a unital
$C^*$-algebra, prove that there is a unique unital isometric
$*$-homomorphism
\[
C(\spec(a))\longrightarrow C^*(1,a),\qquad f\longmapsto f(a),
\]
sending the coordinate function to $a$, and prove the continuous spectral
mapping theorem.
\end{exercise}

\section{Operator Spaces and Operator Tensor Products}

\begin{definition}[Operator spaces and completely bounded maps]
An \emph{operator space} is a closed linear subspace $E\subseteq B(H)$ with
the matrix norms inherited from
\[
M_n(E)\subseteq B(H^{\oplus n}).
\]
For a linear map $u:E\to F$, define
\[
\|u\|_{cb}=\sup_{n\geq1}\|\id_{M_n}\otimes u\|.
\]
The map is \emph{completely bounded} when $\|u\|_{cb}<\infty$ and a
\emph{complete isometry} when every $\id_{M_n}\otimes u$ is isometric.
The dual operator-space structure on $E^*$ is determined by
\[
M_n(E^*)=\operatorname{CB}(E,M_n)
\]
isometrically.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Row and column spaces]
For a Hilbert space $H$, its \emph{column space} and conjugate
\emph{row space} are
\[
H_c=B(\C,H),\qquad \overline H_r=B(H,\C)
\]
with their inherited operator-space structures.
For operator spaces $E$ and $F$, the \emph{Haagerup norm} is
\[
\|u\|_h
=\inf\left\{
\|[x_1\ \cdots\ x_m]\|_{M_{1,m}(E)}
\|[y_1\ \cdots\ y_m]^t\|_{M_{m,1}(F)}:
u=\sum_{j=1}^m x_j\otimes y_j
\right\}.
\]
The completion is denoted $E\otimes_hF$.
The \emph{operator-space projective norm} is
\[
\|u\|_{\wedge}
=\inf\{\|\alpha\|\,\|x\|\,\|y\|\,\|\beta\|:
u=\alpha(x\otimes y)\beta\},
\]
where $x\in M_p(E)$, $y\in M_q(F)$,
$\alpha\in M_{1,pq}$, and $\beta\in M_{pq,1}$.  Its completion is denoted
$E\mathbin{\widehat\otimes}F$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Trace and Hilbert--Schmidt classes]
For $x,y\in H$, let
\[
\theta_{x,y}(\xi)=\langle y,\xi\rangle x.
\]
The algebra $\mathcal K(H)$ of \emph{compact operators} is the norm closure
of the span of the operators $\theta_{x,y}$.
The \emph{trace class} $S_1(H)$ consists of operators admitting a
representation
\[
A=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\theta_{x_j,y_j},
\qquad
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\|x_j\|\,\|y_j\|<\infty,
\]
where the series converges in operator norm, with norm the infimum of the
displayed sums.  Define
\[
\Tr(A)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\langle y_j,x_j\rangle.
\]
The \emph{Hilbert--Schmidt class} $S_2(H)$ consists of operators satisfying
\[
\|A\|_2^2=\sum_j\|Ae_j\|^2<\infty
\]
for an orthonormal basis $(e_j)$ of $H$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Ruan's axioms]
Matrix norms on a complex linear space $E$ satisfy \emph{Ruan's axioms} when
\[
\|x\oplus y\|_{M_{n+m}(E)}
=\max\{\|x\|_{M_n(E)},\|y\|_{M_m(E)}\}
\]
and
\[
\|\alpha x\beta\|_{M_{r,s}(E)}
\leq\|\alpha\|\,\|x\|_{M_n(E)}\,\|\beta\|
\]
for scalar matrices of compatible sizes.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Row--column tensor duality]
Prove that the definitions of $S_1(H)$, $\Tr$, and $S_2(H)$ are independent
of all displayed choices.  Prove the complete isometries
\[
H_c\otimes_h\overline H_r\cong\mathcal K(H),
\qquad
\overline H_r\mathbin{\widehat\otimes}H_c
\cong\overline H_r\otimes_h H_c\cong S_1(H),
\qquad
S_1(H)^*\cong B(H),
\]
where $\overline y\otimes x$ corresponds to $\theta_{x,y}$ and the last
pairing is $(A,T)\mapsto\Tr(AT)$.  Prove that
$\langle A,B\rangle_{S_2}=\Tr(A^*B)$ makes $S_2(H)$ a Hilbert space.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Ruan's representation theorem]
Prove that matrix norms arise from a complete isometry $E\to B(H)$ if and
only if they satisfy Ruan's axioms.  Prove
$M_n(E^*)=\operatorname{CB}(E,M_n)$ completely isometrically.
\end{exercise}

\section{Spectral Theorems from Harmonic Analysis}

\begin{definition}[Projection-valued measures]
A \emph{projection-valued measure} on a measurable space $X$ is a map $E$
from measurable subsets of $X$ to orthogonal projections on $H$ satisfying
\[
E(\varnothing)=0,\qquad E(X)=I,
\qquad
E\!\left(\bigsqcup_{j=1}^\infty B_j\right)
=\sum_{j=1}^\infty E(B_j)
\]
for pairwise disjoint measurable sets, where the sum converges in the strong
operator topology.  The spectral integral $\int_X f\,dE$ for bounded
measurable $f$ is defined by uniform approximation from simple functions.
For a topological space $X$, the measure is \emph{regular} when every
scalar measure $B\mapsto\langle x,E(B)y\rangle$ is a regular Borel measure.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Representations of \texorpdfstring{$C_0(X)$}{C0(X)}]
Let $X$ be locally compact Hausdorff.  A representation
$\pi:C_0(X)\to B(H)$ is \emph{nondegenerate} when
$\overline{\pi(C_0(X))H}=H$.  Prove that every nondegenerate
$*$-representation has a unique regular
projection-valued measure $E_\pi$ satisfying
\[
\pi(f)=\int_X f(x)\,dE_\pi(x),
\qquad f\in C_0(X).
\]
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Bounded normal spectral theorem]
For a normal operator $T\in B(H)$, apply the preceding representation
theorem to the continuous functional calculus
$C(\spec(T))\to B(H)$ and prove that there is a unique projection-valued
measure $E_T$ on $\spec(T)$ satisfying
\[
T=\int_{\spec(T)}z\,dE_T(z),
\qquad
f(T)=\int_{\spec(T)}f(z)\,dE_T(z).
\]
Deduce that a normal operator on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space is
unitarily diagonalizable.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Compact spectral theorem]
Prove that every trace-class operator is compact.  For a compact normal
operator $K$, prove that every nonzero spectral value is an eigenvalue of
finite multiplicity, that the nonzero eigenvalues can accumulate only at
zero, and that
\[
K=\sum_j\lambda_jP_j
\]
in operator norm for pairwise orthogonal finite-rank spectral projections
$P_j$.  For $K\geq0$ in $S_1(H)$, prove
\[
K=\sum_j\lambda_j\theta_{e_j,e_j},
\qquad
\lambda_j\geq0,\qquad
\sum_j\lambda_j=\Tr K.
\]
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Unbounded self-adjoint operators]
An unbounded operator is a linear map $A:\mathcal D(A)\to H$ on a dense
domain.  Its adjoint has domain
\[
\mathcal D(A^*)=
\{y\in H:x\mapsto\langle y,Ax\rangle
\text{ is bounded on }\mathcal D(A)\}.
\]
For $y\in\mathcal D(A^*)$, the vector $A^*y$ is determined by
$\langle A^*y,x\rangle=\langle y,Ax\rangle$ for
$x\in\mathcal D(A)$.  The operator is \emph{self-adjoint} when $A=A^*$,
including equality of domains, and \emph{closed} when its graph is closed
in $H\oplus H$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Unbounded spectral theorem and Stone's theorem]
For a self-adjoint operator $A$, prove that $A\pm iI$ are bijective from
$\mathcal D(A)$ to $H$ with bounded inverses and that the Cayley transform
\[
C_A=(A-iI)(A+iI)^{-1}
\]
is unitary with $\ker(I-C_A)=0$.  Apply the bounded normal spectral theorem
to $C_A$ and the inverse Cayley map to prove that there is a unique
projection-valued measure $E_A$ on $\R$ satisfying
\[
A=\int_\R\lambda\,dE_A(\lambda),\qquad
\mathcal D(A)=
\left\{x:\int_\R\lambda^2\,d\langle x,E_A(\lambda)x\rangle<\infty\right\}.
\]
Use $E_A$ to prove that $e^{-itA}$ is a strongly continuous unitary group
and that every strongly continuous one-parameter unitary group has a unique
self-adjoint generator.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Spectral lines and time evolution]
\label{ex:spectral-lines}
If $A\psi_n=\lambda_n\psi_n$ and an observable $B$ has matrix elements
$B_{mn}=\langle\psi_m,B\psi_n\rangle$, prove
\[
\langle\psi_m,e^{itA/\hbar}Be^{-itA/\hbar}\psi_n\rangle
=e^{it(\lambda_m-\lambda_n)/\hbar}B_{mn}.
\]
Extend the calculation from pure point spectrum to the projection-valued
spectral measure.
\end{exercise}

\section{Ergodic Theory}

\subsection{Probability, conditioning, and entropy}

\begin{definition}[Conditional expectation]
For $X\in L^1(\Omega,\mathcal F,\mathbb P)$ and a sub-$\sigma$-algebra
$\mathcal G\subseteq\mathcal F$, a \emph{conditional expectation}
$\mathbb E[X\mid\mathcal G]$ is a $\mathcal G$-measurable integrable random
variable satisfying
\[
\int_G\mathbb E[X\mid\mathcal G]\,d\mathbb P
=\int_GX\,d\mathbb P
\qquad(G\in\mathcal G).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Shannon entropy]
For a discrete probability vector $p$, its \emph{Shannon entropy} is
\[
H(p)=-\sum_i p_i\log p_i.
\]
The corresponding physical entropy is $S(p)=k_BH(p)$. For probability
vectors $p,q$ with
$p_i=0$ whenever $q_i=0$, their \emph{relative entropy} is
\[
D(p\Vert q)=\sum_i p_i\log\frac{p_i}{q_i}.
\]
For a joint distribution $p_{XY}$, the \emph{mutual information} is
$I(X:Y)=D(p_{XY}\Vert p_Xp_Y)$. For a phase-space probability density $\rho$
relative to a fixed dimensionless normalization of Liouville measure $\mu_L$
(equivalently, to a chosen phase-space cell), its \emph{statistical entropy} is
$S(\rho)=-k_B\int\rho\log\rho\,d\mu_L$; for densities $\rho,\sigma$, set
$D(\rho\Vert\sigma)=\int\rho\log(\rho/\sigma)\,d\mu_L$ when defined.
\end{definition}

\subsection{Time--bandwidth concentration and Gaussian channels}

\begin{definition}[Time and band limiting]
On $L^2(\R)$, define the time-limiting and band-limiting projections by
\[
(Q_Tf)(x)=\mathbf1_{[-T,T]}(x)f(x),
\qquad
B_W=\mathcal F^{-1}\mathbf1_{[-W,W]}\mathcal F,
\]
where $T,W>0$.  The \emph{time--band concentration operator} is
\[
K_{T,W}=Q_TB_WQ_T.
\]
After identifying $Q_TL^2(\R)$ with $L^2([-T,T])$, its normalized
eigenfunctions are the time-limited representatives of the \emph{prolate
spheroidal wave functions}, also called \emph{Slepian functions}.  The
dimensionless parameter $c=TW$ is the time--bandwidth product.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Optimal simultaneous concentration]
Use Plancherel's theorem to prove that $Q_T$ and $B_W$ are orthogonal
projections.  Starting from the Fourier-transform convention in Harmonic
Analysis, derive
\[
(K_{T,W}f)(x)
=\int_{-T}^{T}\frac{\sin W(x-y)}{\pi(x-y)}f(y)\,dy,
\qquad |x|\leq T,
\]
where the diagonal value of the kernel is $W/\pi$.  Prove that $K_{T,W}$
is a positive compact self-adjoint contraction.  If its positive eigenvalues
are $\lambda_0\geq\lambda_1\geq\cdots>0$, use the compact spectral theorem
to prove the Rayleigh--Ritz formula
\[
\lambda_0=\sup_{\|f\|=1}\langle f,K_{T,W}f\rangle,
\]
and hence show
\[
\lambda_0
=\sup_{\substack{f\ne0\\\supp f\subseteq[-T,T]}}
 \frac{\displaystyle\int_{-W}^{W}|\widehat f(\xi)|^2\,d\xi}
      {\displaystyle\int_{\R}|f(x)|^2\,dx}.
\]
Show likewise that $B_WQ_TB_W$ has the same nonzero eigenvalues and that,
for a band-limited eigenfunction $g_n$, $\lambda_n$ is the fraction of its
energy lying in $[-T,T]$.  Thus successive eigenfunctions solve the optimal
concentration problem subject to orthogonality to the preceding solutions.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Slepian modes and Gaussian channel capacity]
Let
\[
A_{T,W}=B_WQ_T:Q_TL^2(\R)\longrightarrow B_WL^2(\R).
\]
Show that $A_{T,W}^*A_{T,W}=K_{T,W}$.  If $f_n$ is a normalized
$\lambda_n$-eigenfunction of $K_{T,W}$, prove that
$g_n=\lambda_n^{-1/2}A_{T,W}f_n$ is normalized, that the $g_n$ are mutually
orthogonal, and that
\[
A_{T,W}f_n=\sqrt{\lambda_n}\,g_n.
\]
Thus the numbers $\sqrt{\lambda_n}$ are the singular gains of the
time--bandwidth channel.

Restrict to the first $N$ real modes and add independent Gaussian noise:
\[
Y_n=\sqrt{\lambda_n}\,X_n+Z_n,
\qquad Z_n\sim\mathcal N(0,N_0),\qquad 0\leq n<N.
\]
For a real random vector with density $p$, write
$h(p)=-\int p\log p$.  Prove nonnegativity of relative entropy for such
densities from $\log u\leq u-1$, and use it to prove that a Gaussian
maximizes $h$ among densities with fixed covariance.  Taking channel
capacity to be the supremum of $I(X:Y)$, show that under the power constraint
$\sum_n\mathbb E[X_n^2]\leq P$ it is, in nats per channel use,
\[
C_N(P)=\max_{\substack{p_n\geq0\\\sum_np_n\leq P}}
 \frac12\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}
 \log\left(1+\frac{\lambda_np_n}{N_0}\right).
\]
Show that independent centered Gaussian inputs attain the maximum and use
Lagrange multipliers to obtain the water-filling allocation
\[
p_n=\left(\nu-\frac{N_0}{\lambda_n}\right)_+,
\qquad \sum_np_n=P.
\]
Explain why $2TW/\pi=\sum_n\lambda_n$ estimates the number of useful modal
channels, while capacity depends on the full eigenvalue distribution, the
noise level, and the power constraint.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Ergodic dynamics and equilibrium states}

\begin{definition}[Ergodic dynamics and dynamical entropy]
A measurable transformation $T$ of a probability space is
\emph{measure preserving} if $\mu(T^{-1}A)=\mu(A)$. Its \emph{Koopman
operator} is the isometry $U_Tf=f\circ T$ on $L^2(\mu)$. The system is
\emph{ergodic} if every invariant measurable set has measure zero or one,
and \emph{mixing} if
\[
\mu(T^{-n}A\cap B)\longrightarrow\mu(A)\mu(B)
\]
for all measurable $A,B$. An \emph{equilibrium state} is an invariant
probability measure for the dynamics.

For a finite measurable partition $\mathcal P$, set
\[
H_\mu(\mathcal P)=-\sum_{A\in\mathcal P}\mu(A)\log\mu(A),
\qquad
h_\mu(T,\mathcal P)
=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n
H_\mu\!\left(\bigvee_{j=0}^{n-1}T^{-j}\mathcal P\right).
\]
The \emph{Kolmogorov--Sinai entropy} is
$h_\mu(T)=\sup_{\mathcal P}h_\mu(T,\mathcal P)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Ergodic averages, mixing, and entropy rate]
Prove that the fixed vectors of $U_T$ are precisely the invariant
$L^2$-observables, and that ergodicity is equivalent to all such observables
being constant almost everywhere. Prove the von Neumann mean ergodic theorem
\[
\frac1N\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}U_T^nf
\longrightarrow P_{\mathrm{inv}}f
\quad\text{in }L^2(\mu),
\]
where $P_{\mathrm{inv}}$ is orthogonal projection onto the invariant
observables. Prove that mixing implies ergodicity and
that it gives decay of correlations
$\int(f\circ T^n)g\,d\mu-\int f\,d\mu\int g\,d\mu\to0$ for bounded
observables.

For every finite partition $\mathcal P$, prove by subadditivity that the
limit defining $h_\mu(T,\mathcal P)$ exists.
For the two-sided Bernoulli shift on $\{1,\ldots,r\}^{\mathbb Z}$ with
product probabilities $p_1,\ldots,p_r$, prove mixing first for cylinder sets
and then for all measurable sets. Show that the time-zero coordinate
partition satisfies
\[
h_\mu(T,\mathcal P)=-\sum_{i=1}^r p_i\log p_i=H(p).
\]
Interpret this equality as the information produced per observed symbol.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Gibbs states]
For a classical Hamiltonian $H$ on a phase space with Liouville measure
$\mu_L$, the \emph{canonical Gibbs state} at inverse temperature $\beta$ is
\[
d\mathbb P_\beta=Z(\beta)^{-1}e^{-\beta H}\,d\mu_L,
\qquad
Z(\beta)=\int e^{-\beta H}\,d\mu_L,
\]
provided $0<Z(\beta)<\infty$. It is stationary under the Hamiltonian
dynamics generated by $H$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Entropy and equilibrium]
\label{ex:classical-gibbs-variational}
For every density $\rho$ with finite mean energy, prove
\[
D(\rho\Vert\rho_\beta)
=-\frac{S(\rho)}{k_B}+\beta\langle H\rangle_\rho+\log Z(\beta).
\]
Deduce that the Gibbs ensemble uniquely maximizes entropy at fixed mean
energy, equivalently minimizes free energy at fixed temperature. Prove
$\langle H\rangle=-\partial_\beta\log Z$ and
$\operatorname{Var}(H)=\partial_\beta^2\log Z$. Prove invariance of Gibbs
measure under Hamiltonian flow. For a finite measure-preserving system,
apply the $L^2$ mean ergodic result proved above to an observable and
identify the long-time mean-square limit in the ergodic case.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Ideal-gas thermodynamics]
Compute the classical canonical partition function of $N$ indistinguishable
noninteracting particles in a box, including the phase-space normalization
$h^{3N}N!$. Derive the ideal-gas law, internal energy, and entropy, and
the one-particle Maxwell--Boltzmann speed distribution. Derive its
temperature scaling and compare both predictions with dilute-gas
measurements in the classical regime.

For a wall of area $A$, express the pressure measured during time $\tau$ as
the time-averaged normal momentum transfer
\[
P_\tau=\frac1{A\tau}
\sum_{\substack{\text{collisions with the wall}\\0<t_j\leq\tau}}
2m|v_{j,\perp}|.
\]
Average the collision flux using the Maxwell--Boltzmann distribution and
recover the same pressure $P=Nk_BT/V$. Identify the stationarity and
ergodic assumptions that relate the long-time reading of one gas sample to
the ensemble calculation. Explain why agreement of pressure, velocity
statistics, and relaxation rates for selected observables and finite times
tests consequences of those assumptions but does not prove that the full
microscopic dynamics is mathematically ergodic.
\end{exercise}

\section{Operator Algebras}

\subsection{Representations and GNS construction}

\begin{definition}[Representations, states, and cyclic representations]
A \emph{representation} of a $C^*$-algebra $A$ is a $*$-homomorphism
$\pi:A\to B(H)$.  A \emph{state} is a positive linear functional of norm
one.  A representation is \emph{cyclic} when there is a vector
$\Omega\in H$ such that $\overline{\pi(A)\Omega}=H$.
For a state $\varphi$, define
\[
N_\varphi=\{a\in A:\varphi(a^*a)=0\},
\qquad
\langle[a],[b]\rangle_\varphi=\varphi(a^*b)
\]
on $A/N_\varphi$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[GNS theorem]
Prove that $N_\varphi$ is a left ideal, that the displayed form is an inner
product, and that left multiplication extends to a cyclic representation
$(\pi_\varphi,H_\varphi,\Omega_\varphi)$ satisfying
\[
\varphi(a)=
\langle\Omega_\varphi,\pi_\varphi(a)\Omega_\varphi\rangle.
\]
Prove uniqueness up to cyclic unitary equivalence.  For a discrete group,
apply the construction to the coefficient-at-the-identity state on
$C_r^*(G)$ and recover the left regular representation on $\ell^2(G)$.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Noncommutative probability}

\begin{definition}[Noncommutative probability]
A \emph{noncommutative probability space} is a unital $*$-algebra $A$
together with a linear functional $\varphi:A\to\C$ satisfying
$\varphi(a^*a)\geq0$ and $\varphi(1)=1$. Its random variables are elements
of $A$, and the joint
$*$-distribution of $X_1,\ldots,X_r$ is the functional
\[
p\longmapsto
\varphi\bigl(p(X_1,\ldots,X_r,X_1^*,\ldots,X_r^*)\bigr)
\]
on noncommutative $*$-polynomials. For a self-adjoint variable $X$, its
moments are $\varphi(X^n)$. A \emph{tracial probability space} also satisfies
$\varphi(ab)=\varphi(ba)$. Random Hermitian matrices form such spaces after
combining expectation with the normalized trace.
\end{definition}

\subsection{Quantum states and dynamics}

\begin{definition}[Quantum states and observables]
A \emph{density operator} on $H$ is a positive element
$\rho\in S_1(H)$ with $\Tr\rho=1$.  An \emph{observable} is a self-adjoint
operator $A$ with
spectral measure $E_A$. The \emph{Born probability} of an event $B$ is
$\Tr(\rho E_A(B))$. A closed-system \emph{unitary evolution} is
$\rho(t)=U_t\rho(0)U_t^*$, where $(U_t)_{t\in\R}$ is a strongly continuous
one-parameter unitary group. For unbounded observables and generators,
spectral measures, expectations, and exponentials use the earlier unbounded
spectral calculus and its natural domains.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Quantized harmonic oscillator]
\label{ex:quantum-oscillator}
For $H=P^2/(2m)+m\omega^2Q^2/2$, define creation and annihilation operators
on the Schwartz core $\mathcal S(\R)$ and prove there that $[a,a^*]=1$ and
$H=\hbar\omega(a^*a+1/2)$. Construct the eigenstates with
$E_n=\hbar\omega(n+1/2)$ and use the spectral-lines exercise to obtain the
frequency spacing $\omega$. Prove that their span is a core for the
self-adjoint closure of $H$. For a two-level internal transition of frequency
$\Omega_0$ coupled linearly to $Q$, compute the oscillator matrix elements,
obtain motional sidebands at $\Omega_0\pm\omega$, and compare their spacing
with trapped-ion spectra.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Liouville and von Neumann equations]
Prove that the Born rule defines a probability measure and that its expected
value is $\Tr(\rho A)$ when defined.

Let $\mu_t=(\phi_t)_*\mu_0$ have smooth density $p_t$ relative to the
Liouville measure $\mu_L$ of a time-independent Hamiltonian system. Using
Exercise~\ref{ex:liouville-theorem}, prove
\[
\partial_t p_t+\{p_t,H\}=0,
\qquad
\frac{d}{dt}\int_M f\,d\mu_t
=\int_M\{f,H\}\,d\mu_t.
\]
For $U_t=e^{-itH/\hbar}$, set
$\rho_t=U_t\rho_0U_t^*$. Prove
\[
\dot\rho_t=-\frac{i}{\hbar}[H,\rho_t].
\]
Prove
\[
\int_M f\,d\mu_t=\int_M(f\circ\phi_t)\,d\mu_0,
\qquad
\Tr(\rho_tA)=\Tr(\rho_0U_t^*AU_t),
\]
and prove conservation of expected energy in both theories.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Blackbody radiation from quantized Fourier modes]
For electromagnetic radiation in a large conducting cavity, use its Fourier
modes and two polarizations to derive the mode density
$g(\omega)=\omega^2/(\pi^2c^3)$ per unit volume. Show that classical Gibbs
equipartition assigns mean energy $k_BT$ to each mode and produces the
ultraviolet divergence. Using the oscillator levels derived in
Exercise~\ref{ex:quantum-oscillator}, compute the quantum Gibbs partition
function and the thermal mean energy
$\hbar\omega/(e^{\beta\hbar\omega}-1)$. Deduce Planck's law
\[
u(\omega,T)=\frac{\hbar\omega^3}{\pi^2c^3}
 \frac{1}{e^{\hbar\omega/(k_BT)}-1},
\]
the Stefan--Boltzmann $T^4$ law, and Wien scaling
$\omega_{\max}\propto T$. Compare the low- and high-frequency limits with
the cavity spectra discussed by
\href{https://doi.org/10.1002/andp.19013090310}
{Planck, \emph{Annalen der Physik} \textbf{309} (1901)}, and identify
precisely where quantization removes the classical divergence.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Fermionic statistics and the CAR algebra}

\begin{definition}[CAR algebra and fermionic Fock space]
For a complex Hilbert space $K$, the \emph{canonical anticommutation
relations algebra} $\operatorname{CAR}(K)$ is the unital $C^*$-algebra
generated by conjugate-linear symbols $a(f)$ satisfying
\[
\{a(f),a(g)\}=0,\qquad
\{a(f),a(g)^*\}=\langle f,g\rangle1.
\]
Let $\Lambda_2^nK$ be the Hilbert-space completion of the $n$th
antisymmetric tensor power of $K$, with $\Lambda_2^0K=\C$. The
\emph{antisymmetric Fock space} is the Hilbert direct sum
\[
\mathcal F_-(K)=\bigoplus_{n\geq0}\Lambda_2^nK.
\]
Its vacuum is $\Omega=1\in\Lambda_2^0K$; creation by $f$ is exterior
multiplication, and annihilation by $f$ is its Hilbert-space adjoint.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[CAR and GNS predict fermionic antibunching]
Prove that creation and annihilation on $\mathcal F_-(K)$ satisfy the CAR and
that $\omega_0(x)=\langle\Omega,x\Omega\rangle$ is a state. Construct the
cyclic map $[x]\mapsto x\Omega$ from its GNS pre-Hilbert space and prove that
its completion is the Fock representation.
Let $\omega_C$ be a gauge-invariant quasifree state with
one-particle density $0\leq C\leq1$, and use its determinantal Wick rule to
prove, for orthonormal detector modes $f,g$, that
\[
\omega_C(N_fN_g)
=\omega_C(N_f)\omega_C(N_g)-|\langle f,Cg\rangle|^2,
\qquad N_f=a(f)^*a(f).
\]
Deduce $N_f^2=N_f$ and fermionic antibunching. Compare the sign and
separation dependence of the correlation deficit with the neutral-atom
measurements in
\href{https://doi.org/10.1038/nature05319}
{F\"olling et al., \emph{Nature} \textbf{444} (2006)}.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Von Neumann algebras and noncommutative conditioning}

\begin{exercise}[Macroscopic magnetization and von Neumann closure]
Let $\mathcal A_N=M_2(\C)^{\otimes N}$, include $\mathcal A_N$ in
$\mathcal A_{N+1}$ by $a\mapsto a\otimes1$, and form the quasilocal spin
algebra
\[
\mathcal A=\overline{\bigcup_{N\geq1}\mathcal A_N}^{\|\cdot\|}.
\]
For $Z=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)$, let $Z_j$ act as $Z$ at site $j$ and set
\[
M_N=\frac1N\sum_{j=1}^N Z_j.
\]
Prove $\|M_{2N}-M_N\|=1$, so $(M_N)$ has no norm limit in $\mathcal A$.

Let $|+\rangle,|-\rangle$ be the $Z$-eigenvectors and define product states
on local elementary tensors by
\[
\omega_\pm(a_1\otimes\cdots\otimes a_N)
=\prod_{j=1}^N\langle\pm|a_j|\pm\rangle.
\]
Prove that these extend to states on $\mathcal A$. In their GNS triples
$(H_\pm,\pi_\pm,\Omega_\pm)$, prove
\[
\pi_+(M_N)\longrightarrow I_{H_+},\qquad
\pi_-(M_N)\longrightarrow-I_{H_-}
\]
in the strong operator topology. First prove the convergence on vectors
$\pi_\pm(a)\Omega_\pm$ with $a\in\mathcal A_k$, using
\[
\|[M_N,a]\|\leq\frac{2k}{N}\|a\|,
\]
and then extend it to all vectors using $\|M_N\|\leq1$.

On $H_+\oplus H_-$, let $\pi=\pi_+\oplus\pi_-$ and
$\Omega=2^{-1/2}(\Omega_+\oplus\Omega_-)$. Prove that $\Omega$ represents the
mixed state $\omega=(\omega_++\omega_-)/2$ and is cyclic. Show that
\[
\pi(M_N)\longrightarrow
C=I_{H_+}\oplus(-I_{H_-})
\]
strongly, that
$C\in\pi(\mathcal A)''\cap\pi(\mathcal A)'$, and that the spectral
projections $(I\pm C)/2$ select the two magnetized phases. Thus the
$C^*$-algebra describes the common local experiments, while its von Neumann
closure in a chosen state also contains the macroscopic phase observable.
Interpret this as an idealized zero-temperature ferromagnet; in an extremal
low-temperature phase of an interacting ferromagnet, the corresponding
limit is $\pm m_\beta I$ under the appropriate ergodic or clustering
hypotheses.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Noncommutative conditional expectation]
\label{def:noncommutative-conditional-expectation}
For a von Neumann subalgebra $N\subseteq M$, a \emph{conditional
expectation} is a normal positive unital projection $E:M\to N$ satisfying
\[
E(n_1an_2)=n_1E(a)n_2
\qquad(a\in M,\ n_1,n_2\in N).
\]
It is \emph{$\varphi$-preserving} for a normal state $\varphi$ when
$\varphi\circ E=\varphi$. When
$M=L^\infty(\Omega,\mathcal F,\mathbb P)$ and
$N=L^\infty(\Omega,\mathcal G,\mathbb P)$, this recovers classical
conditional expectation onto $\mathcal G$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Conditioning on subalgebras]
Show that classical conditional expectation is positive, unital, contractive,
and satisfies the bimodule identity above. For a normal state $\psi$ on
$N$, prove that
\[
\id_M\mathbin{\overline\otimes}\psi:
M\mathbin{\overline\otimes}N\longrightarrow M
\]
is a normal conditional expectation onto $M\otimes1$ after the canonical
identification, and determine which product states it preserves. If $M$ is a
finite von Neumann algebra with normalized trace $\tau$ and $N\subseteq M$,
show that the orthogonal projection
$L^2(M,\tau)\to L^2(N,\tau)$ restricts to the unique $\tau$-preserving
conditional expectation $M\to N$. Derive the tower property for nested
von Neumann subalgebras.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Factor types, modular theory, and relative entropy}

\begin{definition}[Traces and factor types]
A \emph{finite trace} on a von Neumann algebra is a normal positive functional
$\tau$ satisfying $\tau(ab)=\tau(ba)$. A possibly unbounded \emph{normal
tracial weight} is an additive, positively homogeneous map
$\tau:M_+\to[0,\infty]$ satisfying $\tau(x^*x)=\tau(xx^*)$ and
$\tau(x_\lambda)\uparrow\tau(x)$ whenever $x_\lambda\uparrow x$. It is
\emph{semifinite} if every nonzero $x\in M_+$ majorizes a nonzero
$y\in M_+$ with $\tau(y)<\infty$.

Projections $p,q\in M$ are
\emph{Murray--von Neumann equivalent}, written $p\sim q$, if some $v\in M$
satisfies $v^*v=p$ and $vv^*=q$. A projection $p$ is \emph{finite} if
$q\leq p$ and $q\sim p$ imply $q=p$, and it is \emph{minimal} if its only
subprojections are $0$ and $p$.

A \emph{factor} is a von Neumann algebra with center $\C1$. A factor is
\emph{type I} if it has a nonzero minimal projection, equivalently if it is
isomorphic to some $B(K)$; it is \emph{type II} if it has nonzero finite
projections but no minimal projections; and it is \emph{type III} if it has
no nonzero finite projection. A factor is \emph{hyperfinite} if it is the
weak closure of an increasing union
of finite-dimensional $*$-subalgebras.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Projection dimension and factors]
For a finite factor with normalized trace $\tau$, prove additivity and unitary
invariance of $\tau$ on projections. Relate the possible projection values
to matrix factors and to diffuse finite factors. Prove that $B(K)$ is type I
and that its standard trace is semifinite but is not a finite functional
when $K$ is infinite-dimensional.  Prove that a type-III factor has no
nonzero normal tracial state and no trace-density representation of its
normal states.
Explain why finite-dimensional quantum systems see only type-I factors,
whereas the classification permits genuinely different infinite systems.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Cyclic and separating vectors]
For $M\subseteq B(H)$, a vector $\Omega$ is \emph{cyclic} if
$\overline{M\Omega}=H$ and \emph{separating} if $a\Omega=0$ implies $a=0$.
For such a pair $(M,\Omega)$, define the densely defined antilinear operator
$S_0(a\Omega)=a^*\Omega$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[KMS states]
With the present sign convention, a state $\varphi$ is
\emph{$\beta$-KMS} for a flow $\alpha_t$ if, for analytic $a,b$,
\[
\varphi(a\alpha_{t+i\beta}(b))=\varphi(\alpha_t(b)a).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Finite modular flow and Gibbs equilibrium]
Let $H=H^*$ on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space and
$\rho=Z^{-1}e^{-\beta H}$.  On $B(H)$ define
$\alpha_t(a)=e^{itH}ae^{-itH}$.  Prove directly, first on matrix units in an
energy eigenbasis and then by linearity, that
\[
 \Tr\!\bigl(\rho a\alpha_{t+i\beta}(b)\bigr)
 =\Tr\!\bigl(\rho\alpha_t(b)a\bigr).
\]
Show that the modular flow of $\rho$ is
$a\mapsto\rho^{it}a\rho^{-it}=\alpha_{-\beta t}(a)$ and that
$-\log\rho=\beta H+\log Z$.  Deduce that the same spectral data determine
energy gaps, equilibrium weights, and modular frequencies.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Relative entropy of operator-algebraic states]
Let $\varphi$ and $\psi$ be faithful normal states on $M$, represented by
vectors $\Omega_\varphi$ and $\Omega_\psi$ in standard form. Their
relative Tomita operator is the closure of
\[
S_{\varphi\mid\psi,0}(a\Omega_\psi)=a^*\Omega_\varphi,
\]
and their \emph{relative modular operator} is
\[
\Delta_{\varphi\mid\psi}
=S_{\varphi\mid\psi}^*S_{\varphi\mid\psi}.
\]
The \emph{Araki relative entropy} is the extended nonnegative number
\[
D_M(\varphi\Vert\psi)
=\langle\Omega_\varphi,
  \log\Delta_{\varphi\mid\psi}\,\Omega_\varphi\rangle.
\]
For $M=B(H)$ and faithful density operators $\rho,\sigma$, the standard
Hilbert--Schmidt representation gives
$\Delta_{\rho\mid\sigma}(X)=\rho X\sigma^{-1}$ and hence
\[
D(\rho\Vert\sigma)=\Tr\!\left(\rho(\log\rho-\log\sigma)\right).
\]
\end{definition}

\section{Quantum Information Theory}

\subsection{Measurements, entropy, and tomography}

\begin{definition}[Measurements]
A \emph{positive operator-valued measure} is a map $E$ from measurable events
to positive operators with $E(X)=1$ that is countably additive in the weak
operator topology. In state $\rho$, its outcome probability is
$\Tr(\rho E(B))$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[POVMs and dilation]
Prove that projection-valued measures are POVMs. Prove Naimark's dilation
theorem in finite dimensions, realizing every POVM's outcome probabilities
by a projective measurement on a larger Hilbert space. Show that state
discrimination can require a nonprojective POVM.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Von Neumann entropy]
For a density operator with discrete spectral resolution
$\rho=\sum_{\lambda>0}\lambda P_\lambda$, its \emph{von Neumann entropy} is
\[
S(\rho)=-k_B\Tr(\rho\log\rho)
       =-k_B\sum_{\lambda>0}
          (\operatorname{rank}P_\lambda)\lambda\log\lambda,
\]
with $0\log0=0$; in infinite dimension the value may be $+\infty$.
It is $k_B$ times the Shannon entropy of the outcomes of a projective
measurement in an eigenbasis of $\rho$, and it vanishes exactly for pure
states.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Quadratures, Wigner distributions, and homodyne data]
For one canonical degree of freedom, choose $q_0,p_0>0$ with
$q_0p_0=\hbar$ and set
\[
X=Q/q_0,\qquad Y=P/p_0,\qquad
X_\theta=X\cos\theta+Y\sin\theta,
\]
so that $[X,Y]=i$. For a density operator $\rho$, its \emph{Weyl
characteristic function} is
\[
\chi_\rho(u,v)=\Tr\!\left(\rho e^{i(uX+vY)}\right),
\]
and its \emph{Wigner distribution} is the inverse Fourier transform
\[
W_\rho(x,y)=\frac1{(2\pi)^2}\int_{\R^2}
 \chi_\rho(u,v)e^{-i(ux+vy)}\,du\,dv,
\]
interpreted as a tempered distribution when necessary. If $E_\theta$ is the
spectral measure of $X_\theta$, the \emph{homodyne quadrature distribution}
is
\[
p_\rho(r\mid\theta)\,dr=\Tr\bigl(\rho E_\theta(dr)\bigr).
\]
In balanced optical homodyne detection, the local-oscillator phase selects
$\theta$ and repeated measurements estimate this distribution.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Optical homodyne tomography]
Use Exercise~\ref{ex:heisenberg-harmonic-analysis} and the spectral theorem to
prove the Fourier-slice identity
\[
\widetilde p_\rho(k\mid\theta)
=\int_\R p_\rho(r\mid\theta)e^{ikr}\,dr
=\chi_\rho(k\cos\theta,k\sin\theta).
\]
Deduce, in the distributional sense, that the measured quadrature density is
the Radon transform of the Wigner distribution:
\[
p_\rho(r\mid\theta)=\int_{\R^2}W_\rho(x,y)
 \delta(r-x\cos\theta-y\sin\theta)\,dx\,dy.
\]
Prove that the data for $0\leq\theta<\pi$ determine $\chi_\rho$, hence
$W_\rho$ and $\rho$, and derive the filtered-backprojection formula
\[
W_\rho(x,y)=\frac1{(2\pi)^2}\int_0^\pi\int_\R
 |k|\,\widetilde p_\rho(k\mid\theta)
 e^{-ik(x\cos\theta+y\sin\theta)}\,dk\,d\theta.
\]
Show that every $p_\rho(\,\cdot\mid\theta)$ is a genuine probability density
although $W_\rho$ may be negative. Compute the data and reconstruction for
vacuum, coherent, squeezed, and one-photon states. Explain why the factor
$|k|$ amplifies high-frequency noise and why finite-angle experimental data
require regularization or constrained density-matrix estimation. Compare the
predicted reconstruction of vacuum and squeezed states with
\href{https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevLett.70.1244}
{Smithey et al., \emph{Physical Review Letters} \textbf{70} (1993)}.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Quantum relative entropy]
For finite-dimensional density operators with
$\supp\rho\leq\supp\sigma$, their \emph{quantum relative entropy} is
\[
D(\rho\Vert\sigma)
=\Tr\!\left(\rho(\log\rho-\log\sigma)\right),
\]
and it is $+\infty$ otherwise. It reduces to classical relative entropy
when $\rho$ and $\sigma$ commute.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Entropy and quantum Gibbs states]
Let $K$ be $n$-dimensional, let $P_1,\ldots,P_n$ be mutually orthogonal
rank-one projections with sum $1_K$, and let
$\mathcal D=\operatorname{span}\{P_1,\ldots,P_n\}\cong\C^n$. Identify a
state on $\mathcal D$ with a probability vector $p=(p_i)$ and the density operator
$\rho_p=\sum_i p_iP_i$. Prove
\[
S(\rho_p)=-k_B\sum_i p_i\log p_i,
\]
so von Neumann entropy restricts to classical entropy on a commutative
observable algebra. Prove more generally that
$S(U\rho U^*)=S(\rho)$ for every unitary $U$.

Let $H=H^*\in B(K)$ have at least two distinct eigenvalues, and let the
prescribed energy $E$ lie strictly between its smallest and largest
eigenvalues. Set
\[
\rho_\beta=\frac{e^{-\beta H}}{Z(\beta)},
\qquad
Z(\beta)=\Tr(e^{-\beta H}).
\]
Use the spectral resolution of $H$ to reduce the partition function to a
finite classical Gibbs distribution. Referring to
Exercise~\ref{ex:classical-gibbs-variational} and its variance identity,
prove that there is a unique $\beta\in\R$ for which
$\Tr(\rho_\beta H)=E$.
For an arbitrary density operator $\rho$, now prove the genuinely
noncommutative identity
\[
D(\rho\Vert\rho_\beta)
=-\frac{S(\rho)}{k_B}+\beta\Tr(\rho H)+\log Z(\beta).
\]
Use nonnegativity to obtain the quantum variational principle from the
classical one: $\rho_\beta$ uniquely maximizes entropy at fixed mean energy,
equivalently minimizes free energy at fixed temperature.
Finally, restrict $\rho_\beta$ to the commutative algebra generated by the
spectral projections of $H$ and recover the classical Gibbs probabilities,
including their energy-level degeneracies. If $g_i$ is the degeneracy of
$E_i$ and $p_i$ is the total population of that energy level, derive
\[
\frac{p_i}{p_j}=\frac{g_i}{g_j}e^{-\beta(E_i-E_j)}.
\]
Explain why the degeneracy factor is absent for the ratio of populations of
individual eigenstates, and compare both formulas with measured
level-population ratios.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Composite systems and entanglement}

\begin{definition}[Composite and reduced states]
The Hilbert space of a composite system is $H_A\otimes H_B$, and its normal
states are the density operators on this tensor product. A density operator
is \emph{separable} if it lies in the trace-norm closed convex hull of
product density operators and \emph{entangled} otherwise. The \emph{reduced
state} $\rho_A=\Tr_B\rho$ is determined by
$\Tr(\rho_AA)=\Tr(\rho(A\otimes\id))$ for all bounded $A$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Classical and tensor independence]
Families of $\sigma$-algebras are \emph{independent} when the probability of
every finite intersection of events, one from each family, is the product of
their probabilities. Equivalently, bounded random variables measurable with
respect to distinct families have factorizing mixed expectations.

Commuting unital subalgebras $M_1,M_2\subseteq M$ are independent in the
state $\varphi$ when $\varphi(ab)=\varphi(a)\varphi(b)$ for
$a\in M_1$ and $b\in M_2$. They are \emph{tensor independent} when the
algebra they generate, together with the state, is a spatial tensor product
with a product state.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Wishart matrices and typical entanglement]
Let $G$ be a $d_A\times d_B$ matrix of independent standard complex Gaussian
entries and set
\[
W=GG^*,\qquad \rho_A=\frac{W}{\Tr W}.
\]
Show that $\rho_A$ has the same distribution as the reduced state of a
Haar-random unit vector in $\C^{d_A}\otimes\C^{d_B}$. Using Gaussian or
Haar integration, prove
\[
\mathbb E\Tr(\rho_A^2)
=\frac{d_A+d_B}{d_Ad_B+1}.
\]
Deduce that when $d_B\gg d_A$, a typical reduced state is close to maximally
mixed in Hilbert--Schmidt norm. Interpret this as a random-matrix explanation
of why a typical pure state of a large composite system is highly entangled.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Entanglement entropy and mutual information]
For a pure state $\rho=|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|$ on
$H_A\otimes H_B$, its \emph{entanglement entropy} is
$S_A(\psi)=S(\rho_A)$. For an arbitrary bipartite state, its \emph{quantum
mutual information} is
\[
I(A:B)_\rho=D(\rho\Vert\rho_A\otimes\rho_B).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Schmidt projections and entanglement]
Let $H_A$ and $H_B$ be finite-dimensional. For a pure vector
$\psi\in H_A\otimes H_B$, apply the projection form of the singular-value
decomposition to the contraction map
$C_\psi:H_A^*\to H_B$. If $Q_s$ and $R_s$ are its domain and range
projections, decompose $\psi$ into mutually orthogonal spectral blocks
supported on $(Q_sH_A^*)^*\otimes R_sH_B$. Explain why a rank-one refinement
within a block of multiplicity greater than one is not canonical. Prove that a pure
state is separable exactly when its reduced state has rank one, and that the
two reduced states have the same nonzero spectral values with multiplicities
$\operatorname{rank}Q_s=\operatorname{rank}R_s$. Derive
\[
S_A(\psi)=-k_B\sum_{s>0}
 (\operatorname{rank}Q_s)s^2\log s^2
\]
and prove that it vanishes exactly for separable pure states. Compute the
joint and reduced states, local outcome frequencies, and entanglement entropy
of a Bell state, and compare them with entangled-pair tomography.  For every
bipartite density operator $\rho$, prove
\[
I(A:B)_\rho
=\frac{S(\rho_A)+S(\rho_B)-S(\rho)}{k_B}\geq0,
\]
with equality exactly for product states.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Bell correlations and CHSH]
For a polarization-entangled two-photon state, derive the CHSH bound $2$ for
local hidden-variable models. Choose four polarization angles for which the
quantum correlations attain $2\sqrt2$, and derive the predicted coincidence
frequencies. As experimental input, use the adjusted local-realistic
$p$-value $2.3\times10^{-7}$ reported by
\href{https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevLett.115.250402}
{Shalm et al., \emph{Physical Review Letters} \textbf{115} (2015)}.
Determine at the $5\%$, $1\%$, and five-standard-deviation significance
levels, using the one-sided Gaussian five-sigma threshold
$2.87\times10^{-7}$, whether the local-realistic null is excluded. State the
tested freedom-of-choice and spacelike-separation assumptions and explain why
the result does not exclude arbitrary superdeterministic or signaling models.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Quantum channels and data processing}

\begin{definition}[Positive and completely positive maps]
A linear map $\Phi:A\to B$ between $C^*$-algebras is \emph{positive} if it
preserves positive elements and \emph{completely positive} if every
$\id_{M_n}\otimes\Phi$ is positive. A \emph{quantum channel} is a completely
positive trace-preserving map on trace-class operators, equivalently a normal
unital completely positive map on observables in the adjoint picture.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Stinespring and Kraus representations]
Prove that a unital completely positive map $\Phi:A\to B(H)$ has a
representation
$\Phi(a)=V^*\pi(a)V$. In finite dimensions, when $\Phi=\mathcal N^*$ is the
Heisenberg adjoint of a Schr\"odinger-picture channel $\mathcal N$, derive
the paired Kraus formulas
\[
\mathcal N(\rho)=\sum_iK_i\rho K_i^*,
\qquad
\Phi(a)=\sum_iK_i^*aK_i,
\qquad
\sum_iK_i^*K_i=1.
\]
Prove that every channel is unitary evolution on a larger system followed by
discarding an environment.
Use the bimodule identity to prove that every conditional expectation
introduced earlier is completely positive.
For a finite POVM $(E_x)_x$, prove that
\[
\mathcal M(\rho)=\sum_x\Tr(\rho E_x)|x\rangle\langle x|
\]
is a quantum-to-classical channel, compute its adjoint, and identify its
Stinespring dilation with the Naimark dilation constructed earlier.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Relative entropy and data processing]
Prove the log-sum inequality and deduce $D(p\Vert q)\geq0$, with equality
exactly when $p=q$. For a stochastic map $K(y\mid x)$, prove
\[
D(Kp\Vert Kq)\leq D(p\Vert q).
\]
Interpret a random variable, finite-resolution detector, or forgotten label
as a stochastic map and explain why none can increase distinguishability.
Prove
$I(X:Y)=S(p_X)/k_B+S(p_Y)/k_B-S(p_{XY})/k_B$, and show that it vanishes
exactly when $X$ and $Y$ are independent.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Amplitude damping reproduces spontaneous emission]
For a qubit with ground state $|0\rangle$, excited state $|1\rangle$, and
environment vacuum $|0\rangle_E$, let
\[
\begin{aligned}
V_t|0\rangle&=|0\rangle|0\rangle_E,\\
V_t|1\rangle&=\sqrt{\eta_t}\,|1\rangle|0\rangle_E
 +\sqrt{1-\eta_t}\,|0\rangle|1\rangle_E,
\qquad \eta_t=e^{-t/T_1}.
\end{aligned}
\]
In the Schr\"odinger picture, trace out the environment and derive
\[
K_0=|0\rangle\langle0|+\sqrt{\eta_t}|1\rangle\langle1|,
\qquad
K_1=\sqrt{1-\eta_t}|0\rangle\langle1|,
\qquad
\mathcal N_t(\rho)=K_0\rho K_0^*+K_1\rho K_1^*.
\]
Prove complete positivity, trace preservation, and the semigroup law.
Derive $\rho_{11}(t)=e^{-t/T_1}\rho_{11}(0)$ and
$\rho_{01}(t)=e^{-t/(2T_1)}\rho_{01}(0)$. Compare the predicted decay and the
environmental dependence of $T_1$ with the transmon measurements in
\href{https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevLett.101.080502}
{Houck et al., \emph{Physical Review Letters} \textbf{101} (2008)}.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Quantum error correction and operator systems}

\begin{definition}[Quantum codes and correctable errors]
Let $V:K\to H$ be an isometry between finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. Its
image $C=\im V$, with projection $P=VV^*$, is a \emph{quantum code}; $K$ is
the logical system and $H$ the physical system. Write $\mathcal T(H)$ for
the trace-class operators on $H$. A channel
$\mathcal N:\mathcal T(H)\to\mathcal T(H')$ is \emph{correctable on $C$} if
there is a recovery channel $\mathcal R$ such that
\[
(\mathcal R\circ\mathcal N)(V\rho V^*)=V\rho V^*
\]
for every state $\rho$ on $K$. A \emph{stabilizer code} is the common
$+1$ spectral subspace of commuting self-adjoint unitaries. When
$H=\bigotimes_{j=1}^nH_j$, the \emph{distance} of $C$ is the least number
of tensor factors supporting an operator $E$ for which $PEP$ is not a
scalar multiple of $P$, or $+\infty$ if there is no such operator.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Knill--Laflamme correction and decoupling]
Let $\mathcal N(\rho)=\sum_aE_a\rho E_a^*$ and let $P$ project onto a code
$C$. Prove that $\mathcal N$ is correctable on $C$ exactly when there is a
positive semidefinite matrix $(\lambda_{ab})$ such that
\[
PE_a^*E_bP=\lambda_{ab}P
\qquad\text{for every $a,b$}.
\]
Show that this condition is independent of the chosen Kraus representation,
and construct a recovery from the polar decomposition of the combined error
map
\[
W:C\otimes\ell^2(\{a\})\longrightarrow H',\qquad
W(\psi\otimes\delta_a)=E_a\psi,
\]
together with the spectral projections of $(\lambda_{ab})$. Deduce that a
code of distance $d$ corrects every error supported on at most
$\lfloor(d-1)/2\rfloor$ tensor factors.

For an encoding $V:K\to H_A\otimes H_B$, prove that erasure of $B$ is
correctable exactly when the complementary channel
$\rho\mapsto\Tr_A(V\rho V^*)$ is constant on logical states. Interpret this
as the equivalence between recovery from $A$ and absence of encoded
information in $B$; see
\href{https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevA.55.900}
{Knill--Laflamme, \emph{Physical Review A} \textbf{55} (1997)}.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Operator systems]
An \emph{operator system} is a closed self-adjoint linear subspace
$S\subseteq B(H)$ containing the identity. Its matrix order is the family of
cones
\[
M_n(S)_+=M_n(S)\cap B(H^{\oplus n})_+.
\]
Completely positive maps are precisely the maps that preserve these cones at
every matrix level.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[The confusability operator system of a channel]
Let $H,H'$ be finite-dimensional and let
$\mathcal N:\mathcal T(H)\to\mathcal T(H')$ be a channel with Kraus
operators $E_a:H\to H'$. Its \emph{confusability operator system} is
\[
S_{\mathcal N}=\operatorname{span}\{E_a^*E_b:a,b\}\subseteq B(H).
\]
It is self-adjoint, and it contains the identity because
$\sum_aE_a^*E_a=1_H$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Protected observable algebras]
Let $P$ project onto a code $C\subseteq H$. A unital $*$-subalgebra
$\mathcal A\subseteq PB(H)P$, whose identity is $P$, is \emph{protected} or
\emph{correctable} for $\mathcal N$ if there is a recovery channel
$\mathcal R:\mathcal T(H')\to\mathcal T(H)$ such that
\[
P(\mathcal R\circ\mathcal N)^*(A)P=A
\qquad(A\in\mathcal A).
\]
Write
$\mathcal A'=\{X\in PB(H)P:[X,A]=0\text{ for all }A\in\mathcal A\}$ for
the commutant in the code corner.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Exact operator-algebra quantum error correction]
Prove that $\mathcal A$ is correctable for $\mathcal N$ if and only if
\[
[PE_a^*E_bP,A]=0
\qquad(a,b,\ A\in\mathcal A),
\]
or, equivalently,
\[
PS_{\mathcal N}P\subseteq\mathcal A'.
\]
Derive necessity by applying a Stinespring dilation to the recovery identity,
and prove sufficiency by decomposing the finite-dimensional algebra and
constructing a recovery on each summand. For
$\mathcal A=PB(H)P\cong B(C)$, use
$\mathcal A'=\C P$ to recover the ordinary Knill--Laflamme equations
$PE_a^*E_bP=\lambda_{ab}P$. Show that a commutative algebra protects a
classical label, while a decomposition
\[
\mathcal A\cong\bigoplus_\alpha
\bigl(B(H_\alpha^A)\otimes 1_{H_\alpha^B}\bigr)
\]
protects hybrid information: the central label $\alpha$ is classical, the
$H_\alpha^A$ factor is quantum, and $H_\alpha^B$ is an unprotected gauge
subsystem. Compare with
\href{https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevA.76.042303}
{B\'eny--Kempf--Kribs, \emph{Quantum Error Correction of Observables},
Physical Review A \textbf{76} (2007)}.
\end{exercise}

\section{Operator-Space Applications}

\subsection{Channel discrimination}

\begin{definition}[Ancilla-assisted channel discrimination]
A \emph{one-use discrimination strategy} for channels
$\Phi_1,\ldots,\Phi_m$ prepares a state on $H\otimes K$, applies
$\Phi_j\otimes\id_K$ to the unknown channel input, and measures the output
with a POVM. The strategy is \emph{ancilla assisted} when $\dim K>1$. It is
\emph{unambiguous} when every conclusive outcome identifies the channel
without error. If channel $\Phi_j$ is selected with prior probability
$\eta_j$, its \emph{inconclusive probability} $P_I$ is the prior-weighted
average probability of an inconclusive outcome.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[An ancilla improves measurement-channel discrimination]
For $0<\theta<\pi/4$, define
\[
\begin{aligned}
|\phi\rangle&=\cos\theta|0\rangle+\sin\theta|1\rangle,&
|\psi\rangle&=\cos\theta|0\rangle-\sin\theta|1\rangle,
\end{aligned}
\]
and let $\mathcal M,\mathcal N$ be the two projective measurement channels
with rank-one projection families
$\{P_\phi,P_{\phi^\perp}\}$ and
$\{P_\psi,P_{\psi^\perp}\}$. Assume that the two channels are selected with
equal prior probabilities. Use a singlet probe, retain the second qubit as
an ancilla, and condition on the classical measurement outcome. Reduce the
task to unambiguous discrimination of
$|\phi\rangle$ and $|\psi\rangle$ and prove
\[
P_I^{\mathrm{anc}}=\cos(2\theta),\qquad
P_I^{\mathrm{sep}}=\frac{1+\cos^2(2\theta)}2
\]
for the optimal ancilla-assisted and single-qubit strategies. Deduce the
strict advantage for $0<\theta<\pi/4$ and express it as a distinction between
the first and amplified matrix levels of the measurement channels. Compare
the predicted success and inconclusive probabilities with
\href{https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevA.90.022317}
{Mikov\'a et al., \emph{Physical Review A} \textbf{90} (2014)}.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Equivariant compression and Toeplitz operator systems}

\begin{definition}[Equivariant compression]
Let a compact group $G$ act on a Hilbert space $H$ by a unitary
representation $U$, and let a unital $C^*$-algebra $A\subseteq B(H)$ be
invariant under conjugation by $U_g$.  If a finite-rank orthogonal projection
$P$ commutes with every $U_g$, define
\[
S_P=PAP=\{PaP:a\in A\}\subseteq B(PH).
\]
The restricted representation $U^P_g=U_g|_{PH}$ acts on $S_P$ by
conjugation.  We call $S_P$ an \emph{equivariant compression} of $A$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Rotation-covariant Toeplitz compression]
\label{ex:toeplitz-compression}
Let $A=C(S^1)$ act by multiplication on $H=L^2(S^1)$, with normalized
Fourier basis $e_k(\theta)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{ik\theta}$.  Let $P_N$ project
onto $H_N=\operatorname{span}\{e_k:-N\leq k\leq N\}$ and set
$S_N=P_NAP_N$.  Prove
\[
\langle e_j,P_NM_fP_Ne_k\rangle=\widehat f(j-k),
\qquad -N\leq j,k\leq N,
\]
and deduce that $S_N$ is exactly the operator system of
$(2N+1)$-dimensional Toeplitz matrices.

For $z(\theta)=e^{i\theta}$, put $T_N=P_NM_zP_N$.  Show that $T_N$ is a
truncated shift and that $T_NT_N^*$ has nonconstant main diagonal.  Conclude
that $S_N$ is not an algebra, although the $C^*$-algebra generated by $S_N$
is the full matrix algebra $B(H_N)$.

Finally, let rotations act by
$(U_\phi\xi)(\theta)=\xi(\theta-\phi)$.  Prove that $P_N$ commutes with
$U_\phi$ and that conjugation by $U_\phi|_{H_N}$ preserves $S_N$.  Verify
directly that the cutoff retains rotation covariance although its compressed
observables do not form an algebra.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Extension, tensor norms, and bilinear forms}

\begin{exercise}[Finite-dimensional Arveson--Wittstock extension]
Prove in finite dimensions that a completely positive map from an operator
system extends completely positively to the containing $C^*$-algebra.
Let $E\subseteq A$ be an operator space in a finite-dimensional
$C^*$-algebra and let $u:E\to B(H)$ be completely bounded. Deduce, by the
$2\times2$ operator-system construction, that $u$ extends to a map
$\widetilde u:A\to B(H)$ satisfying
$\|\widetilde u\|_{cb}=\|u\|_{cb}$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Operator-space tensor products]
The \emph{minimal operator-space tensor norm} on $E\otimes F$ is inherited
from concrete inclusions in $B(H\otimes K)$. The \emph{Haagerup norm} is
\[
\|z\|_h=\inf\{\|[x_1\ \cdots\ x_r]\|\,
\|[y_1\ \cdots\ y_r]^T\|:z=\sum_ix_i\otimes y_i\}.
\]
A bilinear form $u:E\times F\to\C$ is \emph{jointly completely bounded}
when the maps
\[
u_n([x_{ij}],[y_{kl}])=
 [u(x_{ij},y_{kl})]_{(i,k),(j,l)}
\]
from $M_n(E)\times M_n(F)$ to $M_{n^2}$ are uniformly bounded; the supremum
of their norms is $\|u\|_{jcb}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Tensor norms linearize bilinear maps]
Prove the universal property of the projective Banach-space tensor norm.
Prove that the Haagerup tensor product linearizes completely bounded
factorizations and compare the minimal, Haagerup, and projective norms on
finite-dimensional matrix spaces.
\end{exercise}

\subsection{Nonlocal games as operator-space tensors}

\begin{definition}[Bell functionals and nonlocal games]
A \emph{Bell functional} is an array $M=(M_{xy}^{ab})$ evaluated on
conditional distributions by
$\langle M,p\rangle=\sum_{a,b,x,y}M_{xy}^{ab}p(a,b\mid x,y)$. A two-player
\emph{nonlocal game} $G=(\pi,V)$ consists of a question distribution
$\pi(x,y)$ and a payoff $0\leq V(a,b\mid x,y)\leq1$. Its classical and
entangled values $\omega(G)$ and $\omega^*(G)$ are the optimal expected
payoffs using, respectively, shared randomness and local POVMs
$\{E_a^x\}_a,\{F_b^y\}_b$ on a shared finite-dimensional state $\rho$, with
$p(a,b\mid x,y)=\Tr(\rho(E_a^x\otimes F_b^y))$. An \emph{XOR game} has binary
answers and scores only their parity, equivalently a correlation
$\sum_{x,y}M_{xy}\mathbb E[a_xb_y]$ for signs $a_x,b_y$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Magic-square pseudotelepathy]
The referee chooses $(x,y)\in\{1,2,3\}^2$ uniformly. Alice returns a row
$a\in\{\pm1\}^3$ with product $+1$, Bob returns a column
$b\in\{\pm1\}^3$ with product $-1$, and they win when $a_y=b_x$. Prove by
multiplying all row and column constraints that no deterministic classical
strategy wins every question, construct one losing on only one question,
and hence show $\omega(G_{\rm MS})=8/9$. Using two Bell pairs and a
Mermin--Peres square of commuting two-qubit Pauli observables, construct a
strategy with $\omega^*(G_{\rm MS})=1$.

Compare the ideal query-by-query predictions with the hyperentangled-photon
experiment of
\href{https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevLett.129.050402}
{Xu et al., \emph{Physical Review Letters} \textbf{129} (2022)}. Explain
separately the locality and fair-sampling assumptions in that experiment.
Show why a score above $8/9$ is a device-independent witness under the
stated assumptions.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[The tensor of a game]
Set
\[
E_{X,A}=\ell_1^X(\ell_\infty^A),\qquad
\|z\|_{E_{X,A}}=\sum_x\max_a|z_{x,a}|,
\]
with its standard dual operator-space structure. The tensor associated to
$G=(\pi,V)$ is
\[
\widehat G=\sum_{x,y,a,b}\pi(x,y)V(a,b\mid x,y)
 (e_x\otimes e_a)\otimes(e_y\otimes e_b)
 \in E_{X,A}\otimes E_{Y,B}.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Game values as Banach and operator-space norms]
Identify deterministic strategies with extreme points of the relevant unit
balls and prove
\[
\omega(G)=\|\widehat G\|_{E_{X,A}\otimes_\varepsilon E_{Y,B}},\qquad
\omega^*(G)=\|\widehat G\|_{E_{X,A}\otimes_{\min}E_{Y,B}}.
\]
Equivalently, show that these are the bounded and completely bounded norms
of the bilinear payoff form on the dual mixed-norm spaces. Locate in the
proof the use of positivity of a game tensor, and explain why the same
simple formula need not hold for an arbitrary signed Bell functional. Use
\href{https://doi.org/10.1063/1.4938052}
{Palazuelos--Vidick, \emph{Survey on Nonlocal Games and Operator Space
Theory}, \emph{Journal of Mathematical Physics} \textbf{57} (2016)} as a
guide.
\end{exercise}


\section{Random Matrix Theory}

\begin{definition}[Scalar determinants, Vandermonde determinant, and Dyson classes]
For $A=(a_{ij})\in M_N(\C)$, define
\[
\det A
=\sum_{\sigma\in S_N}\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)
\prod_{i=1}^N a_{i,\sigma(i)}.
\]
For $\lambda=(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_N)$, define
\[
\Delta(\lambda)
=\det[\lambda_i^{j-1}]_{i,j=1}^N
=\prod_{i<j}(\lambda_j-\lambda_i).
\]
Let $\mathbb F=\R,\C$, or $\mathbb H$ and set
\[
\beta=\dim_\R\mathbb F\in\{1,2,4\}.
\]
The Gaussian orthogonal, unitary, and symplectic ensembles, denoted GOE,
GUE, and GSE, consist of the self-adjoint matrices $H=H^*$ over
$\R,\C$, and $\mathbb H$, respectively, with density
\[
d\mathbb P_{N,\beta}(H)
=C_{N,\beta}
\exp\!\left[-\frac{\beta N}{4\sigma^2}\Tr(H^2)\right]dH.
\]
Here $H^*$ is conjugate transpose and $dH$ is Lebesgue measure on the
independent real coordinates.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Eigenvalue--eigenvector Jacobian]
Diagonalize $H=U\operatorname{diag}(\lambda)U^{-1}$ in each Dyson class.
Use the invariant metric $\Tr(dH^2)$ to prove
\[
dH=C\,|\Delta(\lambda)|^\beta
\prod_{i=1}^N d\lambda_i\,d\mu(U),
\]
with the stabilizer and permutation factors included in $C$.  Integrate
over the eigenvectors and derive
\[
p_{N,\beta}(\lambda)
=\frac1{Z_{N,\beta}}
\exp\!\left[-\frac{\beta N}{4\sigma^2}
\sum_{i=1}^N\lambda_i^2\right]
\prod_{i<j}|\lambda_i-\lambda_j|^\beta.
\]
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Beta ensembles and Coulomb gases]
For an interval $I\subseteq\R$, a confining potential $V$, and
$\beta>0$, the \emph{beta ensemble} is
\[
d\mathbb P_{N,\beta,V}(\lambda)
=\frac1{Z_{N,\beta,V}}
\prod_{i<j}|\lambda_i-\lambda_j|^\beta
\prod_{i=1}^N e^{-\beta NV(\lambda_i)/2}\,d\lambda_i.
\]
Its \emph{Coulomb-gas Hamiltonian} is
\[
\mathcal H_N(\lambda)
=\frac N2\sum_{i=1}^NV(\lambda_i)
-\sum_{i<j}\log|\lambda_i-\lambda_j|,
\qquad
d\mathbb P_{N,\beta,V}=Z^{-1}e^{-\beta\mathcal H_N}\,d\lambda.
\]
The Jacobi beta ensemble is the Selberg law on $[0,1]^N$, and the circular
beta ensemble is
\[
\frac1{Z_{N,\beta}^{\rm circ}}
\prod_{i<j}|e^{i\theta_i}-e^{i\theta_j}|^\beta
\prod_{i=1}^N\frac{d\theta_i}{2\pi}.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Logarithmic equilibrium and the semicircle law]
For the empirical measure $L_N=N^{-1}\sum_i\delta_{\lambda_i}$, derive
the mean-field energy
\[
\mathcal I_V(\mu)
=\int V(x)\,d\mu(x)
-\iint\log|x-y|\,d\mu(x)d\mu(y).
\]
Prove that its minimizer satisfies
\[
V(x)-2\int\log|x-y|\,d\mu_V(y)=\ell
\]
on its support.  For $V(x)=x^2/(2\sigma^2)$, solve the singular integral
equation and obtain
\[
\rho_{\rm sc}(x)
=\frac1{2\pi\sigma^2}
\sqrt{4\sigma^2-x^2}\,
\mathbf1_{[-2\sigma,2\sigma]}(x).
\]
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Wigner matrices and empirical spectral measures]
A \emph{Wigner matrix} $H_N$ is an $N\times N$ Hermitian random matrix
whose upper-triangular entries are independent and centered, with
$\mathbb E|H_{ij}|^2=\sigma^2/N$ off the diagonal and, for every fixed
$m$,
\[
\sup_{N,i,j}\mathbb E|\sqrt N H_{ij}|^m<\infty.
\]
Its \emph{empirical spectral measure} is
\[
L_{H_N}=\frac1N\sum_{j=1}^N\delta_{\lambda_j(H_N)},
\qquad
\int x^k\,dL_{H_N}(x)=\frac1N\Tr(H_N^k).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Wigner semicircle law]
Expand $N^{-1}\mathbb E\Tr(H_N^k)$ as a sum over closed index walks.
Prove that only noncrossing pair-matched walks survive as $N\to\infty$
and that their number is the Catalan number.  Prove that the variance tends
to zero and conclude that $L_{H_N}$ converges in probability, in moments,
to $\rho_{\rm sc}(x)dx$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Unfolded spectra and level repulsion]
For a smooth approximation $\overline N(E)$ to the cumulative level count,
define
\[
x_j=\overline N(E_j),
\qquad
s_j=x_{j+1}-x_j.
\]
The variables $x_j$ are the \emph{unfolded levels}.  Their local mean
spacing is one.  Exact symmetry sectors are unfolded separately.  The
factor $|\Delta(\lambda)|^\beta$ defines \emph{level repulsion} of order
$\beta$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Wigner surmises and repulsion exponents]
For $2\times2$ GOE and GUE matrices, separate the trace from the traceless
coordinates and compute the radial distribution of the eigenvalue gap.
After rescaling to unit mean spacing, derive
\[
P_1(s)=\frac\pi2s e^{-\pi s^2/4},
\qquad
P_2(s)=\frac{32}{\pi^2}s^2e^{-4s^2/\pi}.
\]
Use the beta-ensemble density to prove
\[
P_\beta(s)\sim c_\beta s^\beta
\qquad(s\downarrow0),
\]
and compare it with the Poisson spacing law $e^{-s}$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Global, bulk, and edge scales]
Order the eigenvalues as $\lambda_1\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_N$.  Define the
classical location $\gamma_j$ by
\[
\frac jN=\int_{-2\sigma}^{\gamma_j}\rho_{\rm sc}(x)\,dx.
\]
At $E\in(-2\sigma,2\sigma)$, the bulk coordinate is
\[
u=N\rho_{\rm sc}(E)(\lambda-E),
\]
and at the upper soft edge it is
\[
\xi=\frac{N^{2/3}}{\sigma}(\lambda-2\sigma).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[Bulk and edge scales]
Use the expected count $N\rho_{\rm sc}(E)dE$ to derive the bulk spacing
scale $[N\rho_{\rm sc}(E)]^{-1}$.  Prove
\[
\rho_{\rm sc}(2\sigma-s)
\sim\frac1{\pi\sigma^{3/2}}\sqrt{s}
\qquad(s\downarrow0),
\]
and derive the edge scale $s\asymp\sigma N^{-2/3}$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Airy kernel and the Tracy--Widom law]
Let $\operatorname{Ai}$ be the solution of $y''=xy$ decaying at
$+\infty$.  Define
\[
K_{\rm Ai}(x,y)
=\frac{\operatorname{Ai}(x)\operatorname{Ai}'(y)
-\operatorname{Ai}'(x)\operatorname{Ai}(y)}{x-y}
\]
by continuity on the diagonal.  The GUE Tracy--Widom distribution is
\[
F_2(s)=\det(I-K_{\rm Ai})_{L^2(s,\infty)}.
\]
\end{definition}

\subsection{Correlation functions and point processes}

\begin{definition}[Point processes and correlation functions]
Let $(X,\mu)$ be a locally compact measure space.  A \emph{simple point
process} is a random locally finite counting measure
\[
\Xi=\sum_i\delta_{x_i}
\]
with distinct points almost surely.  Its $k$-point correlation function
$\rho_k$ is defined by
\[
\mathbb E\sum_{i_1,\ldots,i_k}^{\ne}
f(x_{i_1},\ldots,x_{i_k})
=\int_{X^k}f(x_1,\ldots,x_k)\rho_k(x_1,\ldots,x_k)
\prod_{j=1}^k d\mu(x_j).
\]
